Abstract AffordanceWAM uses affordance representations from human videos to jointly predict future scenes and robot actions, improving manipulation transfer without human action labels.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Generalizable robot manipulation requires predicting how a scene will evolve, identifying where interactions are feasible, and determining how to act. Action-labeled robot videos directly supervise control but are costly and limited in diversity, whereas egocentric human videos capture diverse interactions but lack robot actions and differ in embodiment and appearance. We introduce AffordanceWAM, an affordance-aware generative World Action Model that represents object-centric spatiotemporal affordance through Scalar Affordance and Affordance Heatmap, within the generated future World. This representation grounds visual prediction in task-relevantant objects and interaction regions for action generation, and provides shared interaction targets across human and robot videos. Built on a pretrained video diffusion Transformer, AffordanceWAM uses separately parameterized World and Action Experts, coupled through Masked Joint Self-Attention, to jointly predict future RGB observations, Scalar Affordance fields, Affordance Heatmaps, and continuous robot actions under a unified flow-matching objective. Human videos supervise all three future-World streams, whereas robot trajectories additionally provide action supervision, enabling transfer without human action labels or retargeting. Experiments on RoboCasa, CALVIN ABCrightarrowD, and real-world manipulation demonstrate consistent gains over RGB-only and robot-data-only baselines. Under fixed robot supervision, RoboCasa performance improves monotonically as affordance-annotated human video scales. These results support affordance as an effective interface for both vision-language-action learning and human-to-robot transfer.
[20.10.2026 05:42] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается AffordanceWAM — генеративная World Action Model, которая учитывает аффордансы объектов, то есть где и как возможно взаимодействие, через Scalar Affordance и Affordance Heatmap. Модель построена на предобученном video diffusion Transformer с раздельными World и Action Experts, связанными через Masked Joint Self-Attention, и совместно предсказывает будущие RGB-кадры, аффордансы и непрерывные действия робота в рамках единого flow-matching objective. Видео людей используются для обучения предсказанию будущего сцены без разметки действий, а траектории робота дополнительно дают action supervision, что позволяет переносить навыки от человека к роботу. Abstract Manga2Animate uses paired creator and reviewer agents to build script and storyboard representations from manga panels, yielding more faithful and consistent animations.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Adapting existing manga into coherent animation that stays faithful to the source is a valuable yet challenging task. Existing methods either access the manga mainly through text, losing its characters and art style, or feed manga pages directly into video generation models, which misinterpret the manga's structure and produce incorrect storylines. In this paper, we propose Manga2Animate, a multi-agent framework that grounds every stage of generation in the source manga. We decompose the task into two intermediate representations, an animation script and a visual storyboard. Each representation is produced by a creator agent and reviewed against the source panels by a paired reviewer agent. We tailor representation-specific selection and dual-level feedback to progressively refine both before synthesizing the final animation. Extensive experiments and a user study demonstrate that Manga2Animate produces animations more faithful to the source manga and more consistent across shots than state-of-the-art approaches. Project page: https://annchenn.github.io/Manga2Animate/

Каждое представление создаётся агентом-создателем (creator agent) и проверяется парным агентом-рецензентом (reviewer agent), который сверяет его с исходными панелями, а отбор и двухуровневая обратная связь (dual-level feedback) постепенно уточняют оба артефакта. Abstract Interlock synthesizes long-horizon tasks as typed completion graphs to enforce cross-component constraints, then uses agent-based verification and self-training to improve agent performance on complex benchmarks.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language model agents increasingly undertake long-horizon tasks in complex environments characterized by fragmented evidence, partially completed states, and dependencies across resources and artifacts. Existing agents may satisfy individual requirements while failing to maintain these relationships, producing locally correct but incomplete outcomes. These failures arise because task completion depends on relationships among environmental evidence, consequential decisions, and required effects. To address this completion gap, we propose Interlock, a framework for task synthesis that represents each task as a typed completion graph. Grounding edges connect public evidence to decisions, coupling hyperedges impose joint constraints across components, and realization edges connect decisions to all required downstream effects. Interlock compiles this graph into a resettable workspace, a public request, and an evaluation rubric. A source-coverage review checks whether the original requirements survive generation, while a public-input review checks whether the rubric is supported by the materials available to the solver. An agent-as-judge inspects execution artifacts and runs verification scripts, retaining trajectories judged to satisfy all mandatory requirements. To evaluate training utility, we fine-tune two Qwen3.6 backbones on their own accepted trajectories. Across the two backbones, training improves AutomationBench task pass rates by 4.00-4.50 percentage points, JobBench weighted scores by 3.20-6.64 points, and Harvey LAB criterion pass rates by 3.89-4.16 percentage points. These results demonstrate the training utility of the synthesized experience across external task environments.
[20.10.2026 05:42] Response: {"desc": "Статья представляет Interlock — фреймворк для синтеза долгосрочных задач для LLM-агентов, где каждая задача описывается как типизированный граф завершения с рёбрами обоснования, гиперрёбрами совместных ограничений и рёбрами реализации. Граф компилируется в воспроизводимое рабочее пространство, публичный запрос и рубрику оценки, а проверка покрытия и публичных входных данных гарантирует корректность генерации. Агент-судья (agent-as-judge) отбирает траектории, удовлетворяющие всем обязательным требованиям, и они используются для дообучения (fine-tuning) двух бэкбон-моделей Qwen3.6 на собственном опыте. Дообучение повышает показатели успешности на бенчмарках AutomationBench, JobBench и Harvey LAB на несколько процентных пунктов.", "emoji": "🧩", "title": "Агенты должны доводить задачи до конца, а не только выполнять их части"}

Abstract The MaSS framework improves self-supervised monocular depth estimation across diverse scenes by separating structural representations from contextual features and using a scene-structure discrimination objective, enabling scalable mixed-domain training and strong zero-shot generalization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Self-supervised monocular depth estimation is appealing for its potential to scale with unlabeled data, yet existing methods often degrade when trained on diverse scenes. This study finds that, under self-supervision, models tend to encode dataset-specific structural biases rather than transferable scene geometry, leading to cross-domain optimization conflicts. To address this issue, this study proposes the MaSS framework, aiming to make self-supervised depth learning scalable to diverse data. MaSS introduces a dedicated structural representation pathway explicitly decoupled from contextual features, which is guided by a novel scene-structure discrimination objective to learn generalized, layout-aware structural representations for depth decoding. Trained and evaluated on four diverse datasets, both individually and jointly, MaSS substantially outperforms prior self-supervised methods and is the first to consistently benefit from mixed-domain training. Zero-shot evaluation on eight additional datasets further demonstrates its remarkable generalizability, particularly on those entirely out-of-distribution scenes. The source code is publicly available at https://mias.group/MaSS.
[20.10.2026 05:42] Response: {"desc": "Статья посвящена самообучаемой оценке глубины по одному монокулярному изображению, которая хорошо масштабируется на неразмеченных данных, но обычно ухудшается при обучении на разнородных сценах. Авторы показывают, что модели под самообучением запоминают специфичные для датасета структурные смещения, а не переносимую геометрию сцены, что приводит к конфликтам оптимизации между доменами. Предложенный фреймворк MaSS выделяет отдельный путь структурных представлений, отделённый от контекстных признаков, и обучает его с помощью новой цели дискриминации структуры сцены. Модель превосходит прежние методы, впервые стабильно выигрывая от смешанного обучения на разных доменах, и демонстрирует сильную zero-shot генерализацию на восьми дополнительных датасетах.", "emoji": "📐", "title": "Учим модель видеть геометрию сцены, а не привычки датасета"}

"Abstract The MaSS framework improves self-supervised monocular depth estimation across diverse scenes by separating structural representations from contextual features and using a scene-structure discrimination objective, enabling scalable mixed-domain training and strong zero-shot generalization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Self-supervised monocular depth estimation is appealing for its potential to scale with unlabeled data, yet existing methods often degrade when trained on diverse scenes. This study finds that, under self-supervision, models tend to encode dataset-specific structural biases rather than transferable scene geometry, leading to cross-domain optimization conflicts. To address this issue, this study proposes the MaSS framework, aiming to make self-supervised depth learning scalable to diverse data. MaSS introduces a dedicated structural representation pathway explicitly decoupled from contextual features, which is guided by a novel scene-structure discrimination objective to learn generalized, layout-aware structural representations for depth decoding. Trained and evaluated on four diverse datasets, both individually and jointly, MaSS substantially outperforms prior self-supervised methods and is the first to consistently benefit from mixed-domain training. Zero-shot evaluation on eight additional datasets further demonstrates its remarkable generalizability, particularly on those entirely out-of-distribution scenes. The source code is publicly available at https://mias.group/MaSS."

Abstract AdaExploreBench evaluates whether LLM agents can actively discover hidden environmental rules and adapt to unannounced changes, revealing that current models struggle to seek relevant evidence and detect stale knowledge.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in unfamiliar and evolving environments, requiring them to proactively explore, understand, and adapt to environmental dynamics. However, existing benchmarks rarely test whether agents can proactively discover how an environment works and adapt as it evolves. To address this gap, we introduce AdaExploreBench, a benchmark for proactive exploration and test-time adaptation in evolving environments. By instantiating environmental dynamics as hidden rules, AdaExploreBench requires agents to actively acquire evidence and infer the rules. Under implicit rule drift, the latent rule changes without announcement, requiring agents to detect from degraded performance that prior knowledge is stale and re-explore. Experiments on eight leading LLMs show that proactive and adaptive exploration remain challenging: GPT-6 Sol achieves only 61.9% calibrated accuracy, while providing sufficient evidence or removing rule drift improves average performance significantly by close to 10%. These results reveal a critical bottleneck in current LLM agents: they can often reason effectively from informative evidence once it is available, but struggle to actively acquire the right evidence and recognize when previously acquired evidence is no longer valid.

Скрытые правила среды не объявляются явно, а агент должен сам собирать доказательства и выводить эти правила. В сценарии неявного дрейфа правил агент должен по снижению качества понять, что его знания устарели, и провести повторное исследование. Эксперименты на восьми ведущих LLM показали, что даже лучшая модель достигает лишь 61,9% калиброванной точности, а предоставление достаточных доказательств или устранение дрейфа заметно повышает результат, что выявляет ключевое узкое место: модели плохо собирают нужную информацию и не замечают, когда старые данные перестали быть валидными.", "emoji": "🔍", "title": "Агенты умеют рассуждать, но не умеют сами искать нужные доказательства и замечать устаревшие знания"}

Abstract A framework called Stateful Harness separates rule-based world state updates from frozen video rendering to improve consistency in interactive world models with hidden state, evaluated via the WorldState-Bench benchmark.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Existing world models can generate action-conditioned video from a single image. Many focus on camera movement, allowing users to explore the generated scene. However, navigation alone does not ensure that an action follows persistent rules when its outcome depends on state that is invisible in the current frame. We present Stateful Harness, a framework that separates world evolution from video rendering to support stateful and rule-governed interaction. It maintains an evolving world state under specified rules, renders each resulting event with a frozen video model, and uses the generated clip to update the world state for the next turn. At each turn, the harness resolves the action selected by the user and compiles the resulting event into an instruction, while a preview clip from an editable 3D scene provides spatial and camera guidance. To evaluate rule compliance, we build WorldState-Bench, a benchmark of 50 counterfactual twin pairs that share an initial frame and action but differ in hidden state and required outcome. Under rule-only prompting, the best of the 11 evaluated video backends produces the required endings for both cases in 32% of pairs. For MiniMax-H3, specifying the required endings raises this rate from 32% to 64%, while the full harness reaches 92% with the same frozen model. These results support separating rule execution from video rendering for world interactions with hidden state.

На каждом шаге harness определяет действие пользователя, формирует инструкцию для генерации, а сгенерированный клип используется для обновления world state. Для оценки соблюдения правил авторы создали WorldState-Bench из 50 контрфактуальных пар с одинаковым начальным кадром, но разным скрытым состоянием. Результаты показывают, что такое разделение заметно повышает долю корректных исходов: с 32% до 92% для модели MiniMax-H3.", "emoji": "🎬", "title": "Правила решают, видео лишь показывает: разделяем логику мира и рендеринг"}

"Abstract A framework called Stateful Harness separates rule-based world state updates from frozen video rendering to improve consistency in interactive world models with hidden state, evaluated via the WorldState-Bench benchmark.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Existing world models can generate action-conditioned video from a single image. Many focus on camera movement, allowing users to explore the generated scene. However, navigation alone does not ensure that an action follows persistent rules when its outcome depends on state that is invisible in the current frame. We present Stateful Harness, a framework that separates world evolution from video rendering to support stateful and rule-governed interaction. It maintains an evolving world state under specified rules, renders each resulting event with a frozen video model, and uses the generated clip to update the world state for the next turn. At each turn, the harness resolves the action selected by the user and compiles the resulting event into an instruction, while a preview clip from an editable 3D scene provides spatial and camera guidance. To evaluate rule compliance, we build WorldState-Bench, a benchmark of 50 counterfactual twin pairs that share an initial frame and action but differ in hidden state and required outcome. Under rule-only prompting, the best of the 11 evaluated video backends produces the required endings for both cases in 32% of pairs. For MiniMax-H3, specifying the required endings raises this rate from 32% to 64%, while the full harness reaches 92% with the same frozen model. These results support separating rule execution from video rendering for world interactions with hidden state."

Abstract JEPA latent-world interpretability should shift from decoding representations to analyzing how latent variables interact, evolve, and causally drive prediction through grounding, structuring, verifying, and acting.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Emerging Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) are moving beyond representation learning towards latent world models that support prediction, planning, and control. Yet their latent worlds naturally lack a human-interpretable interface. In this Perspective, we argue that as JEPA latents become internal states for prediction and action, interpretability must move beyond what they encode to how latent variables interact, evolve, and causally drive prediction. To this end, we envision a four-stage research agenda, involving Ground, Structure, Verify, and Act, to guide this shift toward actionable latent-world understanding. Within our framework, existing JEPA interpretability efforts provide early evidence for Ground, while systematic analysis of latent structure, causal verification, and intervention remains largely unexplored. Overall, this Perspective aims to call for a broader latent-world interpretability agenda that shifts attention from decoding representations toward understanding, validating, and acting on latent worlds.

Abstract CoPE improves agentic reinforcement learning by training policies to generate multi-step action sequences from trajectory histories, increasing sample efficiency without inference overhead.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose CoPE, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, CoPE trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, τ^2-bench, and AppWorld), CoPE (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to 1.9times and 2.0times higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.
[20.10.2026 05:42] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается метод CoPE для обучения языковых агентов с подкреплением (agentic RL), который использует промежуточные шаги траекторий. Вместо только терминального скалярного reward модель учится генерировать последовательности будущих действий на основе истории траектории, формируя временной ансамбль целей. Эти многошаговые action targets обучают общий backbone политики, и агент продолжает действовать пошагово при инференсе без дополнительных затрат. На пяти бенчмарках (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, τ²-bench, AppWorld) CoPE превосходит GRPO и другие RL-базлайны, при этом до 1.9x и 2.0x повышая sample efficiency по сравнению с ECHO и GRPO.", "emoji": "🧭", "title": "Учим агента планировать будущие шаги, не замедляя действия"}

Abstract RSIFlow is a recursive self-improvement framework that dynamically selects component-specific workflows based on execution feedback and evolves a meta-agent to refine orchestration and skills.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Large language model (LLM) agents have demonstrated strong capabilities in complex tasks, including code generation, tool use, and multi-hop question answering. Existing self-improvement methods enhance agent performance through model optimization, harness evolution, and executable artifact refinement, while recursive self-improvement (RSI) further extends optimization to the improvement mechanisms themselves. However, adapting self-improvement workflows to evolving performance bottlenecks while improving workflow orchestration through execution feedback remains a key challenge. We propose RSIFlow, a Recursive Self-Improvement framework that integrates adaptive workflow orchestration with meta-agent capability evolution. RSIFlow diagnoses performance bottlenecks from task-agent trajectories and environmental feedback to dynamically select component-specific improvement workflows. The meta-agent then distills workflow outcomes into component-specific skills and cross-component principles, evolving its own harness to improve subsequent workflow orchestration. Experiments across six benchmarks and two backbone models show that RSIFlow achieves the SOTA average performance on all benchmarks. Ablation studies further demonstrate the contributions of adaptive workflow orchestration and meta-harness evolution. Code is available at https://github.com/1HUAN1/RSI-Flow.

Prompt: Read an abstract of the ML paper and return a JSON with fields: 'desc': explanation of the paper in Russian (4 sentences), use correct machine learning terms. Abstract VLDA reformulates robotic control as compact transition modeling via a Delta variable that bridges vision-language understanding and action generation, improving long-horizon consistency and generalization.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Robotic manipulation is not merely observation-to-action generation, but the realization of task-specified state changes in the physical world. Existing vision-language-action (VLA) methods directly learn observation-to-action mappings, imposing a substantial learning burden due to the gap between semantic understanding and action execution. Although world-action models (WAMs) improve control through future prediction, modeling complete future states wastes capacity on static appearance and action-irrelevant changes. We propose Vision-Language-Delta-Action (VLDA), a paradigm that reformulates full future prediction as compact actionable transition modeling. Its core is a compact Delta variable that captures latent world-state changes, serving as an interface between vision-language understanding and action generation. VLDA learns Delta through a unified objective composed of the Local Transition Filter (LT-Filter) and Global Transition Anchor (GT-Anchor). LT-Filter captures task-relevant manipulation-centric changes while suppressing static reconstruction biases. GT-Anchor provides coarse future-level regularization to preserve the overall direction of state changes. Delta-Driven Action Refinement (DDAR) further grounds Delta in the action manifold and injects it as a structured seed to guide action denoising. We implement VLDA within LoongForge, an open-source training framework for large-scale foundation and embodied models, using its modular and distributed infrastructure for unified two-stage optimization. Experiments show that VLDA improves long-horizon consistency, robustness, and generalization, validating actionable Delta as an alternative to direct action prediction and full future-state modeling.
[20.10.2026 05:42] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает VLDA — подход к робототехническому управлению, в котором вместо прямого отображения наблюдений в действия модель предсказывает компактную переменную Delta, описывающую изменение состояния мира. Delta служит связующим звеном между пониманием визуальной и языковой информации (vision-language) и генерацией действий. Обучение Delta выполняется через совместную функцию потерь из двух компонентов: LT-Filter, который выделяет манипуляционно значимые изменения, и GT-Anchor, задающий общее направление изменений. Эксперименты показывают улучшение согласованности на длинных горизонтах, устойчивости и обобщения, включая работу в новых условиях.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "Предсказывай не будущее целиком, а то, что нужно для действия"}

Abstract Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery improves agent evaluation efficiency by combining historical response patterns with adaptive task selection to estimate full benchmark scores from small subsets.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Evaluating software agents on a full benchmark is expensive. A small task subset can preserve agent rankings while yielding inaccurate scores. Historical responses provide useful structure, but tasks with identical source responses can have different outcomes for a new target agent. We introduce Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR), which combines source-fitted response factors with uncertain group success rates. Observed target outcomes update the posteriors over the target latent state and group rates; posterior-guided acquisition selects subsequent tasks. Its score-based criterion equals the expected one-step reduction in full-score posterior variance. Observed successes and posterior-predictive counts for unqueried tasks yield a full-score posterior. The final estimate uses its median with source-selected calibration. At a 10% task budget on four task banks from the SWE-bench family, HSPR achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on three banks and the lowest maximum absolute error (MaxErr) on all four among the methods compared. Removing group uncertainty or replacing adaptive queries increases macro MAE in the respective controls. Macro MAE decreases across 5-20% budgets.
[20.10.2026 05:43] Response: {"desc": "В статье предлагается метод Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR) для оценки software agents на полном benchmark без запуска всех задач. Метод объединяет response factors, обученные на исторических ответах, с неопределённостью групповых success rates и обновляет posterior после каждого наблюдения. Адаптивный выбор следующих задач основан на ожидаемом уменьшении дисперсии posterior полного score, а итоговая оценка берётся как медиана с калибровкой. На бюджете 10% задач на наборах из семейства SWE-bench HSPR даёт наименьшую MAE на трёх из четырёх наборов и наименьшую MaxErr на всех четырёх.", "emoji": "🤖", "title": "Оцениваем AI-агентов по малой выборке задач, но точно как по полному benchmark"}

"Abstract Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery improves agent evaluation efficiency by combining historical response patterns with adaptive task selection to estimate full benchmark scores from small subsets.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Evaluating software agents on a full benchmark is expensive. A small task subset can preserve agent rankings while yielding inaccurate scores. Historical responses provide useful structure, but tasks with identical source responses can have different outcomes for a new target agent. We introduce Hierarchical Score Posterior Recovery (HSPR), which combines source-fitted response factors with uncertain group success rates. Observed target outcomes update the posteriors over the target latent state and group rates; posterior-guided acquisition selects subsequent tasks. Its score-based criterion equals the expected one-step reduction in full-score posterior variance. Observed successes and posterior-predictive counts for unqueried tasks yield a full-score posterior. The final estimate uses its median with source-selected calibration. At a 10% task budget on four task banks from the SWE-bench family, HSPR achieves the lowest mean absolute error (MAE) on three banks and the lowest maximum absolute error (MaxErr) on all four among the methods compared. Removing group uncertainty or replacing adaptive queries increases macro MAE in the respective controls. Macro MAE decreases across 5-20% budgets."

Abstract Hybrid language models suffer from competing learning between recurrence and attention layers, and a staged training procedure improves retrieval and overall performance.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Many modern language models have adopted hybrid architectures that incorporate both linear-time recurrence and softmax attention. Modern recurrence layers provide a highly expressive mixing mechanism while ensuring high throughput by compressing contextual information into a fixed-size state, but underperform softmax attention in contextual information retrieval. In this paper, we reveal a Competing Learning phenomenon where different kinds of mixers compete to learn the same function, and when any layer learns a function, it suppresses the corresponding learning signal for other layers. Layer attribution analysis reveals that under typical data distributions, modern recurrence learns retrieval functions faster than softmax attention, causing attention to fail to learn retrieval in most cases. Stopping weight updates in recurrence in the middle of training helps attention learn retrieval. Based on these results, we propose a staged training procedure for pre-training hybrid architectures on natural language. Empirical results on models up to 1B parameters show that our method results in substantial improvement in language modeling loss, common-sense reasoning, as well as a wide range of retrieval-related tasks across different context lengths.

Авторы описывают эффект Competing Learning, при котором разные слои конкурируют за выучивание одной и той же функции. Чтобы attention успевал выучить извлечение информации, предлагается поэтапное обучение: в середине тренировки веса рекуррентных слоев замораживаются. Эксперименты на моделях до 1B параметров показывают улучшение языкового моделирования, здравого смысла и задач на поиск в контексте.", "emoji": "🧠", "title": "Слои конкурируют, а не помогают: заморозь рекуррентность, чтобы attention научился искать"}

Abstract DScout improves speculative decoding throughput by aligning diffusion drafter training and inference with prefix acceptance and latency trade-offs.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Speculative decoding accelerates large language model inference through parallel verification of tokens proposed by a drafter. Diffusion drafters further parallelize proposal generation, but greater proposal capacity or higher mean accepted tokens (MAT) need not yield higher throughput. Because verification accepts only a consecutive prefix, early rejections can render later correct predictions ineffective, limiting MAT gains, while even higher MAT may not improve throughput if per-round latency increases. We introduce DScout, which uses two complementary factorizations to guide diffusion drafter training and inference for higher throughput. For training, DScout factorizes position-wise loss into inclusion, weighting, and prediction loss. This guides the design and combination of mixed-horizon training, intra-block unmasking, and acceptance-weighted loss to align supervision with prefix acceptance and improve MAT. For inference, DScout factorizes throughput as the ratio of MAT to per-round latency, guiding target-conditioning depth, drafter depth, and inference block size selection to identify a high-throughput configuration for each workload. Across six benchmarks, DScout achieves 3.84times and 4.14times mean speedups over autoregressive decoding on Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B, respectively, improving mean speedup over the state-of-the-art baseline by 9.1% and 7.1%. Across models and concurrency settings, our inference configuration policy provides up to 9.75% additional mean speedup over DScout's fixed configuration.
[20.10.2026 05:43] Response: {"desc": "Статья предлагает DScout — метод ускорения speculative decoding для больших языковых моделей (LLM), где диффузионный драфтер предлагает токены, а основная модель их верифицирует. Авторы разбивают функцию потерь на компоненты inclusion, weighting и prediction, чтобы обучение лучше соответствовало принятию префикса и росту mean accepted tokens (MAT). На этапе инференса пропускная способность раскладывается как отношение MAT к латентности раунда, что помогает выбирать глубину драфтера, глубину условия на целевую модель и размер блока. На шести бенчмарках DScout дает ускорение в 3.84x и 4.14x относительно autoregressive decoding на Qwen3-4B и Qwen3-8B и превосходит лучший базовый метод на 9.1% и 7.1%.", "emoji": "🚀", "title": "Быстрее не значит больше токенов: выравниваем драфтер под реальную пропускную способность"}

Abstract TokenWorth is a lightweight online memory framework that learns which past experiences are worth retaining and reusing under tight resource constraints, improving lifelong agent success while reducing token usage.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Lifelong agents continually accumulate and reuse experience to improve after deployment, yet in practice they often operate under tight generation, interaction, and memory budgets. Under such stringent joint constraints, we find that existing lifelong-memory methods can become ineffective, with several sophisticated approaches offering little advantage over simple retrieval or even no-memory agents. This reveals a more fundamental problem: resource scarcity makes each memory decision more consequential. When only a small amount of past experience can be retained or reused and opportunities for reasoning and recovery are limited, retrieving a relevant but ineffective memory can consume scarce context and hinder downstream execution. The key challenge is therefore not simply to retrieve relevant experience, but to determine which experiences are actually worth retaining and reusing. To address this challenge, we propose TokenWorth, a lightweight online memory framework that learns query-dependent memory worth from sparse execution feedback and applies it consistently to memory retrieval and bounded memory management. Across three LifelongAgentBench environments and both local and API-based deployments, TokenWorth achieves the highest task success rate while using the fewest output tokens among the evaluated methods. These results suggest that effective lifelong adaptation under resource constraints depends not on remembering more, but on learning what is worth remembering. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/AIGNLAI/TokenWorth.

Abstract EC2-Bench evaluates edge-cloud collaboration for long-horizon LLM agents, revealing trade-offs among performance, resource use, and privacy, and showing that stronger edge models favor more edge-controlled collaboration.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Edge-cloud collaboration combines the lower inference overhead and greater data locality of edge-side execution with the stronger capabilities of cloud-hosted models. However, as LLM agents increasingly operate in long-horizon scenarios, cloud participation becomes interleaved throughout execution rather than a one-shot decision for each query. Such interleaved participation introduces trajectory-level effects that remain insufficiently understood. To address this gap, we introduce EC2-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution. It provides executable digital-workspace agent tasks and a unified evaluation framework covering task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure. Using this benchmark, we empirically study diverse forms of edge-cloud collaboration. Our study reveals three findings. First, collaboration achieves a favorable balance among task performance, resource usage, and privacy exposure compared with edge-only and cloud-only execution. Second, as edge models become stronger, collaborative execution approaches cloud-only performance, and performance differences across strategies shrink, while privacy exposure remains strongly strategy-dependent. Third, stronger edge capability shifts preferred collaboration from externally controlled cloud participation and direct cloud execution toward more edge-controlled collaboration. Further analyses identify cloud-invocation decision-making and instruction following as important edge-side abilities for effective collaboration. These findings provide a systematic understanding of edge-cloud collaboration in long-horizon agent execution and insights for designing future edge agents that effectively collaborate with cloud models. Code and resources are available at https://github.com/OpenBMB/EC2-Bench.
[20.10.2026 05:43] Response: {"desc": "Авторы представляют EC2-Bench — бенчмарк для оценки совместной работы edge и cloud моделей в долгосрочных LLM-агентах, где облако подключается к выполнению задач многократно, а не один раз. Бенчмарк включает исполняемые задачи в цифровом рабочем пространстве и оценивает качество решения, потребление ресурсов и утечку приватных данных. Эксперименты показывают, что коллаборация даёт лучший баланс между этими метриками, чем работа только на edge или только в cloud. Abstract HandsOn is a cross-embodiment simulation benchmark and platform for dexterous manipulation that evaluates generalization across robotic arms, hands, objects, and composed tasks, revealing substantial gaps in current multi-task policies and foundation-model-based control.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small General-purpose manipulation requires reliable behavior across embodiments, objects, and task demands. We introduce HandsOn, a composable simulation platform and benchmark for cross-embodiment dexterous manipulation. It supports five robotic arms, six dexterous hands, and approximately 200 rigid, articulated, and deformable assets in randomized scenes. The benchmark pairs 18,000 curated human demonstrations with 66 human-verified tasks: Core-50 supports demonstration-based learning and execution tests, while Transfer-16 evaluates asset and relation transfer, skill composition, and deformable manipulation without target-task training demonstrations. Qualitative real-robot rollouts illustrate sim-to-real transfer. Across diffusion, vision-language-action, world-action, and prompted VLM-based methods, the best trained multi-task policy achieves 29.1% Core-50 success. Without benchmark finetuning, GPT-6 using move_eef for palm-and-finger control achieves 5.2% on Core-50 and 1.9% on Transfer-16 over 660 trials. Trained-policy stage diagnostics expose failures in contact establishment, action coordination, and maintaining achieved goals, with additional sequencing errors in composed tasks. HandsOn provides a shared basis for investigating manipulation generalization and the limits of reliable physical interaction.

Abstract QD-Rec uses quality-diversity optimization and foundation models to generate diverse, well-reasoned recommendations for high-stakes decision-making.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Open-ended search combined with foundation models has produced advances such as faster algorithms, new results on open mathematical problems, and self-improving agents. We extend this paradigm to recommendation for decision-making, focusing on decisions where the set of possible choices is fixed but the reasons for choosing them are not. In high-stakes decisions, e.g., which company to acquire or which customer to target, comparing diverse, well-reasoned options can be more useful than a single ranked list. We formalize open-ended recommendation as building a diverse, high-quality set of recommendations, each an item together with a hypothesis, a natural-language reason why the item fits the needs of a decision maker. We propose QD-Rec, a quality--diversity method that selects items with a contextual bandit and generates hypotheses with a foundation model from earlier ones kept in an archive as stepping stones. A new recommendation enters the archive if it is novel or better than its nearest neighbor. Across M&A, customer targeting, and paper and book recommendation, QD-Rec gives the highest mean quality and builds the largest archive in most settings.

Система формирует не один ранжированный список, а разнообразный набор высококачественных рекомендаций, каждая из которых состоит из объекта и гипотезы на естественном языке о его пригодности. Выбор объектов выполняется с помощью contextual bandit, а гипотезы генерируются LLM на основе ранее сохранённых в архиве удачных вариантов, выступающих как «ступеньки». Abstract Transcribed speech retains speaker and demographic information that large language models can exploit for verification, though releasing only brief text excerpts reduces linkage risk.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Speech anonymization has focused on the acoustic signal, leaving open how much speaker information the transcribed words themselves retain. In this work, we study pairwise speaker and demographic-attribute verification from transcribed speech. We introduce SPARC (Spoken Profile Authorship Recognition Corpus), a benchmark built from two podcast corpora comprising over 12,500 speakers, 1.5M quality-filtered utterances, and speaker-level inferred or source-provided labels for sex, age, and accent, and evaluate five attribution models and three LLMs on identity- and attribute-verification trials with episode- and metadata-based topic proxies. LLM adversaries distinguish same-speaker pairs from text alone. Attribute-label agreement is harder to verify, but LLMs perform sex- and accent-label verification above chance. Finally, releasing only short probe texts (50 words) degrades the strongest adversaries the most, offering a partial reduction in observed linkage.

Авторы представляют SPARC — бенчмарк на основе подкастов с более чем 12 500 дикторами и 1,5 млн высказываний, с метками пола, возраста и акцента. Paper text to classify:

"Abstract Transcribed speech retains speaker and demographic information that large language models can exploit for verification, though releasing only brief text excerpts reduces linkage risk.  Generated by thinkingmachines/Inkling-Small Speech anonymization has focused on the acoustic signal, leaving open how much speaker information the transcribed words themselves retain. In this work, we study pairwise speaker and demographic-attribute verification from transcribed speech. We introduce SPARC (Spoken Profile Authorship Recognition Corpus), a benchmark built from two podcast corpora comprising over 12,500 speakers, 1.5M quality-filtered utterances, and speaker-level inferred or source-provided labels for sex, age, and accent, and evaluate five attribution models and three LLMs on identity- and attribute-verification trials with episode- and metadata-based topic proxies. LLM adversaries distinguish same-speaker pairs from text alone. Attribute-label agreement is harder to verify, but LLMs perform sex- and accent-label verification above chance. Finally, releasing only short probe texts (50 words) degrades the strongest adversaries the most, offering a partial reduction in observed linkage."
